A 5m swing only matters when:
  - It forms within a HTF zone of interest (4H/1H OB or FVG)
  - It creates a CHoCH or BOS that confirms a HTF thesis
  - It provides a structural level for SL placement on a
    sniper entry that has full HTF support

A 5m swing in open space (not at any key level) is noise.
Do not build analysis on it.

────────────────────────────────────────
MULTI-SIGNIFICANCE SWING DETECTION
────────────────────────────────────────
A single candle can be a swing at multiple significance levels:

Example: candle[200] on 1H data
  - Swing High with N=2? Yes → minor swing
  - Swing High with N=3? Yes → moderate swing
  - Swing High with N=5? Yes → major swing
  - Swing High with N=8? Yes → dominant swing

The more N values a swing satisfies, the stronger it is.
A swing that passes N=2 but fails N=3 is a minor pivot.
A swing that passes N=5+ is a structural cornerstone.

The system should check multiple N values and tag each swing
with its maximum confirmed N:
  max_N = 2 → minor
  max_N = 3 → moderate
  max_N = 5 → major
  max_N = 8+ → dominant

This directly affects:
  - How much liquidity sits above/below it (more visible = more stops)
  - How likely it is to be swept (dominant swings are prime targets)
  - How significant a break of it is (breaking a dominant swing =
    major BOS vs. breaking a minor swing = possibly inducement)

================================================================================
3.3  SWING QUALITY SCORING
================================================================================

Not all swings are equal. Two candles can both mathematically
qualify as swing highs with N=3, but one may be a clear structural
level while the other is barely distinguishable from noise.

The system evaluates swing quality using 5 factors:

────────────────────────────────────────
FACTOR 1: CONFIRMATION DEPTH (max_N)
────────────────────────────────────────
As described above: how many N values does the swing satisfy?

  max_N = 2 → Score: 1/5
  max_N = 3 → Score: 2/5
  max_N = 4 → Score: 3/5
  max_N = 5 → Score: 4/5
  max_N ≥ 6 → Score: 5/5

────────────────────────────────────────
FACTOR 2: REJECTION QUALITY (wick analysis)
────────────────────────────────────────
How aggressively was the swing level rejected?

For a Swing High, look at the candle AT the swing:
  rejection = upper_wick / range (of the swing candle)

  rejection ≥ 0.50 (wick is 50%+ of range) → Score: 5/5
    Strong rejection. Price reached the high but was pushed back
    forcefully. Indicates significant selling at that level.
  rejection 0.35-0.49 → Score: 4/5
  rejection 0.20-0.34 → Score: 3/5
  rejection 0.10-0.19 → Score: 2/5
  rejection < 0.10 → Score: 1/5
    Minimal wick. Price closed near the high. The "swing" was
    more of a gradual turn than a sharp rejection. Less likely
    to hold if retested.

For a Swing Low: same calculation using lower_wick / range.

────────────────────────────────────────
FACTOR 3: VOLUME AT THE SWING
────────────────────────────────────────
Volume on the swing candle relative to average:

  vol_ratio of swing candle:
  > 2.0 → Score: 5/5 (high participation in the rejection)
  1.5-2.0 → Score: 4/5
  1.0-1.5 → Score: 3/5 (average volume — neutral)
  0.7-1.0 → Score: 2/5 (below average — less conviction)
  < 0.7 → Score: 1/5 (thin market — swing may not hold)

High volume at a swing means many participants agreed that the
level was significant. Low volume means the swing formed due to
lack of interest rather than active rejection — these swings
break more easily.

────────────────────────────────────────
FACTOR 4: AT A SIGNIFICANT LEVEL?
────────────────────────────────────────
Does the swing coincide with a known institutional level?

  At a HTF Order Block (Daily/4H OB) → Score: 5/5
  At a HTF FVG boundary or CE → Score: 4/5
  At a round number (e.g., 100000, 50000) → Score: 3/5
  At a previous swing level (retest) → Score: 3/5
  At EQH/EQL cluster → Score: 4/5
  At Session level (PDH/PDL, Asian H/L) → Score: 3/5
  Not at any notable level → Score: 1/5

A swing that forms at a known institutional zone is far more
likely to be significant and to hold on retest than a swing
that forms in "empty space."

────────────────────────────────────────
FACTOR 5: VISIBILITY ON HIGHER TIMEFRAME
────────────────────────────────────────
Can this swing be identified on the next higher timeframe?

  A 1H swing that is also a 4H swing → Score: 5/5
  A 15m swing that is also a 1H swing → Score: 5/5
  A swing visible only on its own timeframe → Score: 2/5
  A swing visible only as minor (N=2) on its own TF → Score: 1/5

Higher timeframe visibility means more traders see the level,
which means more stops are placed near it, which means more
liquidity, which means institutions are more likely to target it.

────────────────────────────────────────
OVERALL SWING QUALITY RATING
────────────────────────────────────────
Sum the 5 factor scores (each 1-5): Total range 5-25

  21-25: STRONG swing
    High-priority structural level. Very likely to hold on first
    retest. Major liquidity pool. BOS of this swing = significant
    structure break. SL beyond this level = well-protected.

  15-20: MODERATE swing
    Solid structural level. May hold on retest if HTF supports.
    Decent liquidity pool. Worth tracking for structure and
    liquidity analysis.

  10-14: WEAK swing
    Minor structural level. May break easily on retest.
    Small liquidity pool. Do not rely on this swing for major
    structural decisions. Useful for LTF entry timing within
    a HTF-confirmed setup.

  5-9: NOISE
    Barely qualifies as a swing. Likely caused by normal market
    fluctuation. Do not use for structure, liquidity mapping,
    or any decision. Breaking this swing is not BOS.

The system should tag every detected swing with its quality rating
and use it throughout the analysis:
  - MARKET_STRUCTURE: only use MODERATE+ swings for BOS/CHoCH
  - LIQUIDITY_MAPPING: prioritize STRONG swings as sweep targets
  - ENTRY: use WEAK swings for LTF entry SL placement (acceptable
    for execution-level precision within a HTF setup)

================================================================================
3.4  EQUAL HIGHS AND EQUAL LOWS (EQH / EQL)
================================================================================

Equal Highs and Equal Lows are among the most important liquidity
formations in ICT methodology. They represent levels where retail
traders have clustered their stop losses, creating a dense pool
of liquidity that institutions will target.

────────────────────────────────────────
DETECTION FROM OHLC DATA
────────────────────────────────────────
Two (or more) swing highs are "equal" when:

  |swing_high_A.price - swing_high_B.price| / swing_high_A.price < 0.003

This means the two highs are within 0.3% of each other.

Why 0.3% tolerance?
  - Exact same price is rare (markets don't repeat to the pip)
  - 0.1% is too strict (would miss obvious visual equal highs)
  - 0.5% is too loose (would flag highs that aren't truly "equal")
  - 0.3% strikes the balance: captures what a human would visually
    identify as "equal" on a chart

For Equal Lows:
  |swing_low_A.price - swing_low_B.price| / swing_low_A.price < 0.003

────────────────────────────────────────
DOUBLE vs TRIPLE vs MULTIPLE
────────────────────────────────────────
  2 equal swing points = Double Top/Bottom
    Liquidity: moderate
    Probability of sweep: 65-75%

  3 equal swing points = Triple Top/Bottom
    Liquidity: high
    Probability of sweep: 80-90%
    Every retail trader can see three touches = everyone puts
    stops above/below = massive liquidity pool

  4+ equal swing points = Multiple
    Liquidity: very high
    Probability of sweep: 85-95%
    At some point, sweeping this level becomes almost inevitable

The MORE equal touches, the MORE liquidity is concentrated there,
and the HIGHER the probability that institutions will engineer
a move to sweep it.

────────────────────────────────────────
WHY EQH/EQL ARE SO IMPORTANT
────────────────────────────────────────
When retail traders see a "double top" or "triple bottom," they
place trades based on these patterns:
  - Double top: retail places sells with SL above the highs
  - Triple bottom: retail places buys with SL below the lows

These stop losses ARE the liquidity:
  - SL above double top = buy stop orders = Buy-Side Liquidity
  - SL below triple bottom = sell stop orders = Sell-Side Liquidity

Institutions need this liquidity to fill large orders.
To buy a large position, they need sellers → they sweep the BSL
above the double top, triggering those buy stops (which become
market sells for the people being stopped out), providing the
liquidity institutions need to fill their buy orders at favorable
prices. Then price reverses and drops.

This is the core ICT liquidity concept.

────────────────────────────────────────
DETECTION ALGORITHM
────────────────────────────────────────
Step 1: Identify all swing highs for the timeframe (using appropriate N)
Step 2: For each pair of swing highs (A, B):
  Calculate: |A.price - B.price| / A.price
  If < 0.003 AND they are separated by at least 3× N candles:
    → Mark as Equal Highs (EQH)

Step 3: Group into clusters:
  If swing C is also within 0.3% of A and B → Triple EQH
  Continue for any additional swings in the cluster

Step 4: Record:
  - EQH/EQL level = average price of the cluster
  - Count = number of touches (2, 3, 4+)
  - Spread = max - min within the cluster (how "equal" they really are)
  - Time span = candles between first and last touch
  - Status = UNSWEPT or SWEPT

The separation requirement (at least 3× N candles between touches)
prevents flagging consecutive candles at the same level as "equal
highs." The touches must be separate swing events with meaningful
price movement between them — this is what creates the visible
pattern that retail traders react to.

────────────────────────────────────────
EQH/EQL CONTEXT MATTERS
────────────────────────────────────────
Not all EQH/EQL are equally tradeable:

EQH in an uptrend (HH/HL structure):
  - Price is making higher highs, then makes TWO equal highs
  - This suggests the trend is stalling
  - BSL above the EQH is the obvious target
  - BUT: in a strong uptrend, the sweep may lead to continuation
    (price sweeps above, then continues higher — not a reversal)
  - Use as TP target, not necessarily as a reversal zone

EQH at the top of a range / potential distribution:
  - Price has been ranging, EQH forms at the top
  - SSL below has not been taken
  - Classic ICT play: sweep EQH (BSL) → reverse → target SSL
  - This is high-probability reversal setup

EQL in a downtrend:
  - Equal lows forming as price makes lower lows
  - SSL below is the target
  - Sweep may lead to continuation (further down)

EQL at the bottom of a range / potential accumulation:
  - Range + EQL at bottom + BSL above not taken
  - Classic ICT: sweep EQL (SSL) → reverse → target BSL
  - Mirror of the distribution scenario

The system must not blindly assume every EQH/EQL sweep = reversal.
The surrounding structure (trend, HTF bias, which side has unswept
liquidity) determines the expected behavior after the sweep.

================================================================================
3.5  MINOR vs MAJOR SWINGS AND THE INDUCEMENT PROBLEM
================================================================================

Not every swing break is a true Break of Structure. This is one of
the most common errors in ICT analysis and a leading cause of losses.

────────────────────────────────────────
MINOR SWING vs MAJOR SWING
────────────────────────────────────────
Minor Swing:
  Detected with N=2 (or N=3 on LTF)
  Small price excursion between larger structural swings
  Low quality score (typically 5-12)
  Often forms during pullbacks within a larger trend

Major Swing:
  Detected with N=5+ (or N=3+ on HTF)
  Clear structural turning point
  Higher quality score (typically 15+)
  Defines the trend's HH/HL or LH/LL sequence

THE CRITICAL DISTINCTION:

In an uptrend (HH/HL):
  Major swings: the HH points and HL points that define the trend
  Minor swings: the small dips and bounces within each leg

  Breaking a minor swing low (small dip within an up-leg):
  → NOT a CHoCH. NOT a trend change. NOT actionable.
  → This is INDUCEMENT — it looks like a break to trigger
    retail stops, then price continues in the original direction.

  Breaking the MAJOR swing low (the HL that defines the trend):
  → THIS is a CHoCH or BOS. This IS structurally significant.

INDUCEMENT DETECTION:
  Step 1: Identify the major swings (N=5 or highest confirmed N)
  Step 2: Between two major swings, identify minor swings (N=2-3)
  Step 3: If price breaks a minor swing but the next major swing
          remains intact → this is inducement, not a real break
  Step 4: If price breaks a MAJOR swing → this is a genuine
          structural event (BOS if with trend, CHoCH if against)

Example:
  Major HL at $98,000 (N=5 confirmed, quality score 18)
  Minor swing low at $99,200 (N=2, quality score 8)
  Price drops to $99,100 (breaks the $99,200 minor swing)

  This is NOT CHoCH. The major HL at $98,000 is intact.
  This is inducement — retail shorts enter here thinking
  the trend is reversing. Then price bounces from $99,100
  and continues to make a new HH.

  If instead price drops below $98,000 (breaks the major HL):
  THIS is CHoCH. The structural HL that defined the uptrend
  has been violated. Now look for confirmation (HL formation
  in the new direction, BOS down).

────────────────────────────────────────
PRACTICAL RULES
────────────────────────────────────────
1. For BOS/CHoCH analysis: use only MODERATE or STRONG swings
   (quality score 15+, or N=3+ minimum)

2. For liquidity mapping: include WEAK and MINOR swings
   (they still attract stops, even if they're not structural)

3. For SL placement: use the nearest MODERATE+ swing beyond
   the entry zone (not a minor swing that could easily be swept)

4. When a minor swing breaks but the major swing holds:
   → Do not change your structural assessment
   → Consider this inducement (potential entry opportunity if
     price returns to a key zone after triggering minor stops)

5. When a major swing breaks:
   → Update the structural assessment immediately
   → This changes BOS/CHoCH status
   → Reassess bias if this was a HTF major swing

================================================================================
3.6  THE LAG PROBLEM
================================================================================

Swing detection requires candles AFTER the potential swing point
to confirm it. This creates an inherent delay (lag) that the
system must handle honestly.

────────────────────────────────────────
THE PROBLEM
────────────────────────────────────────
A candle is only confirmed as a swing high (with N=3) after 3
subsequent candles have all printed lower highs.

At candle[100], if candle[100].high > candle[97-99].high:
  We need candle[101], [102], [103] to all have lower highs
  before we can confirm candle[100] as a swing high.

Until candle[103] closes:
  - Candle[100] is a POTENTIAL swing high (unconfirmed)
  - It might become confirmed (if 101-103 stay lower)
  - It might be invalidated (if 101, 102, or 103 prints a higher high)

────────────────────────────────────────
HOW THE SYSTEM HANDLES LAG
────────────────────────────────────────
RULE 1: NEVER build structural decisions on unconfirmed swings.

  If the most recent candle could be a swing high but N right-side
  candles have not yet formed → do NOT mark it as a confirmed swing.

  You may note it as "potential swing forming at [price]" with
  reduced confidence, but structural calls (BOS, CHoCH, trend
  determination) must use confirmed swings only.

RULE 2: The current candle and the most recent N-1 candles are
always in the "confirmation zone" — they cannot be swing points yet.

  With N=3 on 1H:
  If the dataset has 500 candles (index 0-499):
  - Candles 0 through 496 can be evaluated for swing status
  - Candles 497, 498, 499 are in the confirmation zone
  - Candle 499 (the current/latest) is definitely unconfirmed

RULE 3: Acknowledge the lag in your analysis.

  "The most recent confirmed swing high is at candle[485] = $101,500.
   Candle[493] shows a higher high at $101,800 but requires 2 more
   candles to confirm as a swing. If confirmed, this would represent
   a new HH in the current structure."

  This is honest. Saying "the swing high is at $101,800" when it's
  unconfirmed is premature and could lead to false structure calls.

RULE 4: Use multiple N values to reduce effective lag.

  A swing confirmed at N=2 (needs 2 right-side candles) has less lag
  than N=5 (needs 5 right-side candles).

  Strategy:
  - Use N=2 for early detection (faster, less reliable)
  - Use N=3 for structural confirmation (standard)
  - Use N=5 for major structural events (slower, most reliable)

  When N=2 detects a swing and N=3 is pending:
  → Note the swing as "early detection, pending confirmation"
  → If N=3 confirms → upgrade to structural swing
  → If N=3 fails (new candle exceeds the level) → discard

────────────────────────────────────────
LAG AND REAL-TIME DECISION MAKING
────────────────────────────────────────
In live analysis, lag means the system is always slightly behind.
This is not a flaw — it is a feature. Waiting for confirmation
prevents reacting to false signals.

The cost of lag: occasionally missing the first few candles of a move
(the entry could have been slightly better if you anticipated the swing).

The benefit of lag: avoiding false signals where what looked like
a swing high was just a pause before continuation higher.

The math overwhelmingly favors waiting for confirmation.
A few ticks of worse entry on confirmed signals is far cheaper
than multiple losses on false signals.

In backtesting, lag is less of an issue because the system has
all candles available and can look back to identify confirmed
swings precisely. The system should still process candles in
sequence (as if it were live) to avoid forward-looking bias.

================================================================================
3.7  SWING POINT DATA STRUCTURE
================================================================================

For each detected swing, the system should record:

  {
    index:          candle index in the dataset
    timestamp:      candle timestamp (for charting reference)
    price:          the high (for SH) or low (for SL) value
    type:           "SWING_HIGH" or "SWING_LOW"
    max_n:          highest N value confirmed (2, 3, 5, 8, etc.)
    quality_score:  sum of 5 factors (5-25)
    quality_rating: "STRONG" / "MODERATE" / "WEAK" / "NOISE"
    at_level:       what significant level it's at, if any
    visible_on_htf: true/false (does it appear on next higher TF?)
    status:         "ACTIVE" / "BROKEN" / "SWEPT"
    eqh_eql_group:  null or group_id if part of an equal cluster
  }

When a swing is broken:
  - Update status to "BROKEN"
  - Record which candle broke it and how (body close vs wick)
  - Determine if this constitutes BOS/CHoCH (see MARKET_STRUCTURE)

When a swing is swept (wick beyond, body returns):
  - Update status to "SWEPT"
  - Record the sweep candle and displacement after
  - This swing's liquidity has been taken (see LIQUIDITY_MAPPING)

This data structure feeds into every subsequent section:
  MARKET_STRUCTURE reads the swing sequence for trend/BOS/CHoCH
  LIQUIDITY_MAPPING uses swing prices as BSL/SSL levels
  ORDER_BLOCKS uses swing breaks to confirm OB validity
  ENTRY_MODELS uses swing levels for SL placement

================================================================================
3.8  SWING DETECTION ACROSS TIMEFRAMES
================================================================================

The system analyzes multiple timeframes simultaneously. Each
timeframe produces its own set of swings with its own N parameter.
These sets interact:

────────────────────────────────────────
ALIGNMENT
────────────────────────────────────────
When a swing on a lower timeframe aligns with a swing on a higher
timeframe (within 0.3% tolerance), the lower TF swing inherits
the higher TF's significance.

Example:
  4H swing high at $101,500 (N=3, quality 17)
  1H swing high at $101,480 (N=3, quality 14)

  These align (within 0.3%). The 1H swing is now tagged as
  "visible on HTF" → Factor 5 score becomes 5/5 → quality
  increases. The level $101,500 area is now a 4H+1H swing
  high = strong liquidity and structural level.

────────────────────────────────────────
NESTING
────────────────────────────────────────
Between two 4H swings, there will be multiple 1H swings.
Between two 1H swings, there will be multiple 15m swings.

This nesting creates the fractal structure that ICT describes:
  4H swing high → 4H swing low = one 4H "leg"
  Within that leg: several 1H swings (internal structure)
  Within each 1H move: several 15m swings (micro structure)

The system must track which LTF swings are WITHIN which HTF
structure. This determines:
  - Whether a LTF swing break is internal (normal) or external
    (breaking the HTF structure — significant)
  - Where to look for entry setups (within the current HTF leg)
  - Where the "line in the sand" is (the HTF swing that must hold
    for the thesis to remain valid)

────────────────────────────────────────
CONSTRUCTING HTF SWINGS FROM LTF DATA
────────────────────────────────────────
In backtesting, the system may need to construct 4H or Daily
candles from 1H data (or lower). When doing this:

  4H candle from four 1H candles:
    open = first 1H candle's open
    high = max of four 1H highs
    low = min of four 1H lows
    close = last 1H candle's close
    volume = sum of four volumes

After construction, run swing detection on the synthetic HTF data
using that timeframe's N parameter (N=3 for 4H).

The synthetic HTF swings should closely match what a native 4H
chart would show. Minor differences may occur at boundaries
(which four 1H candles group into a 4H depends on the start time).
This is acceptable and does not materially affect analysis.

================================================================================
3.9  COMMON MISTAKES IN SWING DETECTION
================================================================================

These are the errors that lead to incorrect structural analysis:

MISTAKE 1: USING N=2 FOR STRUCTURAL DECISIONS
  N=2 swings are useful for entry-level precision but too noisy
  for structure. Calling a CHoCH based on a N=2 swing break will
  generate many false signals. Use N=3 minimum for structure.

MISTAKE 2: TREATING EVERY WICK AS A SWING
  A wick that briefly exceeds a neighbor's high does not always
  create a meaningful swing. Check the quality score. If it's
  below 10, it's likely noise — useful for noting the price level
  but not for structural decisions.

MISTAKE 3: IGNORING THE LAG
  Marking the most recent candle as a confirmed swing because
  "it looks like the high" is forward-looking bias. The system
  does not know if the next candle will make a higher high.
  Wait for confirmation candles.

MISTAKE 4: EQUAL-PRICE CONFUSION
  Two candles with the same high are EQH, not a single swing.
  The system must detect these as an equal cluster, not pick
  one and ignore the other.

MISTAKE 5: WRONG N FOR THE TIMEFRAME
  Using N=5 on 5m data will barely find any swings (too strict).
  Using N=2 on Daily data will find too many (too loose).
  Use the parameters defined in section 3.2.

MISTAKE 6: NOT UPDATING SWING STATUS
  A swing that has been broken or swept must be marked as such.
  Treating a broken swing as still "active" leads to analyzing
  structure that no longer exists. Swing status must be dynamic.

================================================================================
END OF [SWING_DETECTION]
================================================================================



================================================================================
[MARKET_STRUCTURE]
================================================================================

PURPOSE:
Market structure is the answer to the most fundamental question in
trading: "Is price going up, going down, or going nowhere?"

Every ICT concept — Order Blocks, FVGs, liquidity sweeps, entry
models — is meaningless without first knowing the structural context.
A bullish Order Block in a confirmed downtrend is not a buying
opportunity; it is a zone that will likely be broken through. A
liquidity sweep in an uptrend is a buying opportunity; the same
sweep in a downtrend is continuation.

Market structure is determined by the SEQUENCE of swing points
identified in the previous section. The system reads this sequence
and determines:
  1. What is the current trend? (Bullish / Bearish / Ranging)
  2. Has the trend just changed? (CHoCH detected)
  3. Has the trend been confirmed? (BOS detected)
  4. How strong is the current structure?
  5. Is the current movement WITH the trend or a pullback AGAINST it?

This section defines exactly how to make these determinations from
OHLC-derived swing point data, how to distinguish real structural
events from noise, and how to handle the complex reality where
different timeframes show different structures simultaneously.

================================================================================
4.1  TREND IDENTIFICATION FROM SWING SEQUENCES
================================================================================

A trend is a directional bias visible in the sequence of swing
highs and swing lows. It is not a feeling, not a guess, and not
based on a single candle or indicator. It is a mathematical
relationship between confirmed swing points.

────────────────────────────────────────
BULLISH STRUCTURE (UPTREND)
────────────────────────────────────────
Definition:
  Price is making Higher Highs (HH) AND Higher Lows (HL)

Minimum requirement:
  At least 2 consecutive Higher Highs AND 2 consecutive Higher Lows
  using MODERATE or STRONG quality swings (score 15+).

Identification from swing array:
  Given confirmed swings in chronological order:
  SL1, SH1, SL2, SH2, SL3, SH3...

  Bullish if:
    SH2.price > SH1.price (Higher High)
    SL2.price > SL1.price (Higher Low)
    AND this pattern repeats: SH3 > SH2, SL3 > SL2

  One HH + one HL is a POTENTIAL bullish structure.
  Two HH + two HL is a CONFIRMED bullish structure.

  Why the minimum of 2 each?
    A single HH+HL after a downtrend could be a deep pullback
    (bear rally) rather than a genuine reversal. Two consecutive
    sets prove that the pattern is repeating, not a one-off event.

Strength assessment:
  The DISTANCE between consecutive swings matters:

  Strong bullish:
    Each HH is significantly higher than the previous (>1% for BTC)
    Each HL is well above the previous HL
    The impulse legs (SL→SH) are larger than the pullback legs (SH→SL)
    Volume increases on impulse legs, decreases on pullbacks

  Weak bullish:
    HH barely exceeds previous HH (<0.3%)
    HL is barely above previous HL
    Impulse and pullback legs are similar in size
    Volume is flat or declining overall

  Weak bullish structure is vulnerable to reversal. It suggests
  buyers are running out of momentum. Be cautious with new longs
  in weak bullish structure — the next HH attempt may fail,
  creating the setup for a CHoCH.

────────────────────────────────────────
BEARISH STRUCTURE (DOWNTREND)
────────────────────────────────────────
Definition:
  Price is making Lower Highs (LH) AND Lower Lows (LL)

Minimum requirement:
  At least 2 consecutive LH AND 2 consecutive LL
  using MODERATE+ quality swings.

Identification:
  SH2.price < SH1.price (Lower High)
  SL2.price < SL1.price (Lower Low)
  Pattern repeats.

Strength assessment:
  Same logic as bullish but inverted.
  Strong bearish: each LL significantly lower, impulse legs larger
  Weak bearish: LL barely lower, pullback legs nearly equal impulse

────────────────────────────────────────
RANGING STRUCTURE (SIDEWAYS)
────────────────────────────────────────
Definition:
  Price is NOT making a clear HH/HL or LH/LL sequence.

Characteristics:
  - Swing highs are approximately equal (EQH forming)
  - Swing lows are approximately equal (EQL forming)
  - OR swings alternate: HH then LL then HH then LL (no trend)
  - Range = distance between the highest high and lowest low
  - Price oscillates within this range

Identification:
  If the last 4+ swings do not show a consistent HH/HL or LH/LL
  pattern, the structure is RANGING.

  Specific conditions for ranging:
    |SH_latest - SH_previous| / SH_latest < 0.005 (highs similar)
    AND |SL_latest - SL_previous| / SL_latest < 0.005 (lows similar)
    OR: SH2 > SH1 but SL2 < SL1 (expanding, no directional bias)
    OR: SH2 < SH1 but SL2 > SL1 (contracting/converging)

Trading in ranging structure:
  ICT approach to ranges is NOT to trade the range (buy low, sell
  high within it). Instead:
    1. Identify which side has more unswept liquidity
    2. Wait for a sweep of one side (BSL above or SSL below)
    3. Enter after the sweep with displacement in the opposite direction
    4. Target the liquidity on the other side

  A range is accumulation or distribution (see MARKET_MAKER_MODEL).
  The question is: which side will be swept first, and which
  direction will the eventual breakout go?

  The answer comes from HTF bias:
    If HTF (Daily/Weekly) is bullish → range is likely accumulation
    → SSL will be swept first (to grab liquidity for the up move)
    → Then BSL will be the target (breakout upward)

    If HTF is bearish → range is likely distribution
    → BSL swept first → then SSL is the target

────────────────────────────────────────
TRANSITIONAL STRUCTURE
────────────────────────────────────────
The market does not instantly switch from bullish to bearish.
There are transitional periods that the system must recognize:

  Bullish → Transitional → Bearish

  Bullish: HH + HL confirmed, momentum strong
  ↓
  Weakening: HH barely higher, HL barely higher
  ↓
  First warning: HH fails (price makes an equal high or lower high)
  ↓
  CHoCH: price breaks below the last HL
  ↓
  Transition: first LH forms above the CHoCH level
  ↓
  Confirmation: BOS below the low that preceded the CHoCH
  ↓
  Bearish: LH + LL confirmed

  At each stage, the system's confidence in the structure changes:
    Strong bullish: high confidence in bullish bias
    Weakening: reduce confidence, tighten trailing, watch for CHoCH
    CHoCH detected: structural bias shifts to neutral/caution
    First LH forms: tentative bearish bias forming
    BOS confirmed: bearish structure confirmed
    LH + LL repeating: full bearish confidence

================================================================================
4.1B  HOW TO CORRECTLY LABEL A SWING AS HH/HL/LH/LL (WHY THIS GOES WRONG)
================================================================================
This section exists because of a REAL, DOCUMENTED, REPEATED failure mode
observed in actual backtesting (not a theoretical concern): an analyst
(human or AI) looks at a swing high/low, states its correct numeric
price, and STILL labels it wrong (calls a Higher High a "Lower High",
or vice versa). This is not a math error in the sense of "3+3=7" — it
is a CONCEPTUAL error: comparing the current swing to the WRONG
reference point, or no reference point at all, and then trusting a gut
impression instead of doing the actual comparison.

Real documented case (do not repeat this exact mistake):
  An analyst wrote: "lower high formation at candle -2, high = 2200.0"
  The actual previous swing high on the chart was at candle -3,
  price = 2157.23.
  2200.0 is GREATER than 2157.23. This is mechanically, unambiguously,
  a HIGHER HIGH — not a Lower High. The label was the OPPOSITE of what
  the numbers show. This single mislabel flipped an entire directional
  bias from bullish to bearish, and the resulting SELL thesis was wrong
  in a market that actually rose over the following days.

Why does this specific type of error happen? Four distinct root causes,
each requiring a different fix. You must understand WHICH one you are
at risk of before you can avoid it — memorizing "HH means higher than
before" is not enough, because the error is never in the definition,
it is always in HOW the comparison is actually carried out.

────────────────────────────────────────
ROOT CAUSE 1: COMPARING AGAINST THE WRONG REFERENCE POINT
────────────────────────────────────────
The label HH/HL/LH/LL is ALWAYS a comparison between exactly two
things: the swing you are labeling right now, and the most recent
PRIOR CONFIRMED SWING OF THE SAME TYPE (high-vs-high, or low-vs-low,
never mixed). The single most common failure is silently comparing
against the wrong "previous" swing:

  (a) Comparing against a swing of the WRONG kind. You cannot compare
      a high against a low. "Is 2200 higher than the swing LOW at
      2100?" is a meaningless question for labeling a swing HIGH —
      you must find the most recent swing HIGH before it.

  (b) Comparing against a swing that is not actually the IMMEDIATELY
      PRECEDING one of that kind. If there were three swing highs in
      sequence (A at idx -10, B at idx -6, C at idx -2), and you are
      labeling C, you compare C against B (the one immediately before
      it), NOT against A (an older one two steps back), and not
      against some vague "the last big number I remember seeing on
      the chart". Skipping over B and comparing C to A can flip the
      label: C might be higher than A but lower than B (or vice
      versa) — the correct label uses B, always the nearest one.

  (c) Comparing against a MINOR/NOISE-level swing instead of a
      genuine structural one. A tiny 2-candle wiggle that is not truly
      a meaningful turning point should not be used as "the previous
      swing" for structural labeling purposes (see FACTOR breakdown in
      section 3.3 — swings scoring below MODERATE quality are noise
      for structural decisions). If your reference point itself was
      never a real swing, your comparison is meaningless before you
      even do the arithmetic. This is why detect_significant_swings()
      output (injected as PRE-COMPUTED MATH EVIDENCE in your prompt)
      exists — it has ALREADY filtered out swings that are statistically
      indistinguishable from noise (using true topographic prominence,
      normalized by ATR). When that evidence lists significant highs
      and lows, treat those specific points as your default reference
      set for comparison — not any random local wiggle you notice
      visually that isn't on that list.

  (d) Comparing against a swing that is UNCONFIRMED (too close to the
      most recent candle to have proven itself yet — see section 3.6,
      THE LAG PROBLEM). An unconfirmed "candidate" swing can still be
      exceeded by the very next candle and therefore isn't a real
      swing at all yet. Using it as your reference produces a label
      that may not even describe a real structural fact.

THE FIX: Before writing ANY label, explicitly state in your own
reasoning: "The swing I am labeling is at index ___, price ___. The
most recent PRIOR CONFIRMED swing of the SAME TYPE (high-vs-high or
low-vs-low) is at index ___, price ___." Only once BOTH numbers are
explicitly written down do you compare them. If you cannot name the
specific prior swing you are comparing against, you are not ready to
assign a label — go find it first.

────────────────────────────────────────
ROOT CAUSE 2: DOING THE COMPARISON "BY FEEL" INSTEAD OF BY SUBTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
A very common but invisible error: looking at two numbers that are
close together (e.g., 2200.0 and 2157.23 — only about 2% apart) and
letting an impression of "these are basically similar / this looks
like it's chopping sideways / this looks like a pullback failing"
override the actual arithmetic. The human eye (and a language model
generating text token by token) is bad at precise numeric comparison
when skimming a narrative — it is very easy to "feel" that a move
looks weak or range-bound and therefore assume the newer high must be
lower, without actually subtracting the two numbers.

THE FIX: Literally compute new_price - previous_price. If the result
is positive, and you are evaluating a HIGH, it is by definition higher
= HH candidate (or, if the broader trend context is bearish and this
is the first higher high in a while, it may instead signal an early
CHoCH — see section 4.3 — but it is still numerically a higher value,
never call it "lower" because it "feels" unconvincing). If the result
is negative, it is lower = LH candidate. There is no ambiguity once
you have actually subtracted. "It looks like consolidation so this
must be a lower high" is not a valid derivation — the ONLY valid
derivation is the subtraction.

────────────────────────────────────────
ROOT CAUSE 3: CONFUSING TREND EXPECTATION WITH ACTUAL COMPARISON
────────────────────────────────────────
This is the most dangerous failure because it is self-reinforcing. If
you have already concluded "the market is bearish" (perhaps correctly,
from Weekly/Daily context, or from a CHoCH that occurred earlier), it
becomes tempting to label EVERY subsequent high as a "Lower High"
because that is what you EXPECT to see in a bearish structure — even
when the actual price of that specific high is numerically greater
than the swing before it.

This is backwards. The swing labels (HH/HL/LH/LL) are not a checklist
you fill in to match your bias — they are the RAW EVIDENCE that
either confirms or challenges your bias. If, inside an established
bearish trend, a new swing high turns out to be numerically HIGHER
than the prior swing high, that is not a labeling choice you get to
make to preserve your narrative — it is a genuine Higher High, and it
is meaningful information: either (a) this is a normal pullback high
inside the bearish leg that has not yet broken the governing Lower Low
structure (still bearish overall, but this specific high is honestly
a HH on its own terms and should be reported as such, with the
broader bearish context noted separately), or (b) it may be an early
warning sign of a bullish CHoCH forming (see section 4.3) if it also
breaks a key prior structural high. Either way, mislabeling the
number itself to protect a pre-existing bias corrupts everything
downstream — the BOS/CHoCH engine, the liquidity map, and the final
trade decision are all built directly on these labels being honest.

THE FIX: Label the swing using ONLY its numeric relationship to the
correct prior reference point (Root Cause 1). Do this BEFORE deciding
what it means for the overall trend. Determine the label first, purely
mechanically; interpret its significance for bias SECOND, as a
separate step. Never let step two influence step one.

────────────────────────────────────────
ROOT CAUSE 4: TREATING "HIGHER/LOWER" AS A ROUGH IMPRESSION RATHER
THAN A STRICT INEQUALITY
────────────────────────────────────────
By definition (section 4.1): a Higher High requires price_new >
price_previous (strictly greater — not "roughly similar", not "close
enough to call it the same"). If two swing highs are extremely close
(e.g., within 0.1-0.3% of each other), this is NOT automatically a
"Higher High" or a "Lower High" — first check whether it actually
qualifies as an EQUAL HIGH (EQH — see section 3.4) instead. EQH is a
THIRD, DISTINCT classification, not a coin-flip between HH and LH.
Forcing every swing into either "HH" or "LH" when it might genuinely
be an EQH is itself a source of false labels. If it is not an EQH by
the defined tolerance, then apply the strict inequality honestly, no
matter how small the numeric difference is — 2200.01 is still, by
strict definition, higher than 2200.00.

────────────────────────────────────────
A COMPLETE WORKED EXAMPLE (THE EXACT DOCUMENTED FAILURE, CORRECTED)
────────────────────────────────────────
Data: candle at index -3 has high = 2157.23 (a confirmed, MODERATE+
quality swing high — appears in the significant_highs evidence).
Candle at index -2 has high = 2200.0.

WRONG process (what actually happened in the real failure):
  "Price has been forming lower highs recently in a bearish daily
  trend, so this next high at 2200 is probably another lower high."
  → Labeled: "LH at idx -2 (2200.0)". WRONG — no actual subtraction
  was performed; the label was chosen to match the expected narrative.

CORRECT process:
  Step 1 — Identify the reference: "The swing I am labeling is idx -2,
    price 2200.0 (a swing high). The most recent PRIOR CONFIRMED swing
    HIGH is idx -3, price 2157.23."
  Step 2 — Subtract: 2200.0 - 2157.23 = +42.77 (positive).
  Step 3 — Check EQH tolerance: 42.77 / 2157.23 = ~2.0%, far larger
    than any reasonable EQH tolerance (~0.1-0.3%) — not an EQH.
  Step 4 — Apply strict inequality: 2200.0 > 2157.23 → this is
    mechanically a HIGHER HIGH.
  Step 5 — Now interpret significance separately: "This HH occurs
    inside what I believed was a bearish Daily structure. This is
    either (a) a normal pullback high that has not broken the
    governing structure — note it as HH but keep overall bias
    provisional pending what the LOWS do next, or (b) if it also
    breaks the most recent significant swing high with a decisive
    close, this may be the first sign of a bullish CHoCH forming (see
    4.3) — re-examine the bias with this new evidence, do not silently
    discard it because it contradicts the previous conclusion."
  → Correct label: "HH at idx -2 (2200.0), exceeds prior swing high
    at idx -3 (2157.23) by +42.77 (+2.0%) — this HONESTLY CONTRADICTS
    a purely bearish read; re-evaluating bias using this fact rather
    than suppressing it."

────────────────────────────────────────
MANDATORY SELF-CHECK BEFORE STATING ANY HH/HL/LH/LL LABEL
────────────────────────────────────────
Before writing any structural label in your output, run this checklist
literally, in order, every single time — do not skip it because the
label "feels obvious":
  1. What index and price is the swing I am about to label?
  2. What index and price is the most recent PRIOR CONFIRMED swing of
     the SAME kind (high-vs-high or low-vs-low)? Cite it explicitly —
     if you cannot name it, go find it in the provided swing evidence
     before proceeding.
  3. Subtract: new_price - previous_price. What is the sign?
  4. Is the absolute difference small enough to instead be an EQH/EQL
     (section 3.4)? If yes, use that label, not HH/LH or HL/LL.
  5. Apply the strict inequality mechanically. Do NOT let your
     pre-existing view of the trend change this specific label.
  6. Only after the label is fixed, discuss what it MEANS for bias —
     as a separate sentence, never folded into the labeling step
     itself.
  7. If PRE-COMPUTED MATH EVIDENCE (from detect_significant_swings, or
     a MECHANICAL_SWING_CONTRADICTION check) is provided in your
     prompt and it lists a different prior swing price than the one
     you used, TRUST THE PROVIDED EVIDENCE — it was computed directly
     from the raw OHLC array, independent of any narrative reasoning,
     and is not subject to the four root causes above.

This is not about following a rigid formula for its own sake — it is
about the exact discipline a real professional trader uses precisely
BECAUSE the human eye and casual pattern-matching are unreliable for
this specific task. An expert trader does not "feel" that a high is
lower; they check it against the actual, specific, named prior swing,
every time, especially when a pre-existing bias could otherwise bias
the read.

================================================================================
4.2  BREAK OF STRUCTURE (BOS)
================================================================================

BOS is the event that CONFIRMS the current trend is continuing.
It is not a reversal signal — it is a continuation signal.

────────────────────────────────────────
DEFINITION
────────────────────────────────────────
BULLISH BOS:
  In an existing uptrend (HH/HL structure):
  Price's CLOSE breaks ABOVE the most recent swing high.

  This means: the uptrend just made another HH → trend continues.

BEARISH BOS:
  In an existing downtrend (LH/LL structure):
  Price's CLOSE breaks BELOW the most recent swing low.

  This means: the downtrend just made another LL → trend continues.

KEY REQUIREMENT: BOS can only occur WITH the existing trend.
  - Breaking a high in an uptrend = bullish BOS ✓
  - Breaking a low in a downtrend = bearish BOS ✓
  - Breaking a high in a downtrend = NOT BOS, this is CHoCH ✗
  - Breaking a low in an uptrend = NOT BOS, this is CHoCH ✗

────────────────────────────────────────
BOS VALIDATION CRITERIA
────────────────────────────────────────
Not every close beyond a swing point is a valid BOS. The system
applies these validation checks:

CRITERION 1: CLOSE BREAKS, NOT JUST WICK
  The candle's CLOSE must be beyond the swing level.
  A wick beyond the level with close back inside = liquidity sweep,
  not BOS. This is the most critical distinction.

  Bullish BOS: candle.close > swing_high.price
  Bearish BOS: candle.close < swing_low.price

  If only candle.high > swing_high but candle.close < swing_high:
  → This is a sweep/raid of BSL, NOT a BOS.
  → Watch for reversal (this is a potential short setup, not bullish
    continuation).

CRITERION 2: CANDLE QUALITY
  The breaking candle should show conviction:
    body_pct > 50% (more than half the range is body)
    Ideally body_pct > 60% for strong BOS

  A BOS candle with 20% body and 80% wicks suggests the break
  was marginal and may not hold. This is a weak BOS.

  Exception: if the break happens across 2-3 candles (gradual
  push through the level), body_pct of individual candles matters
  less. What matters is that CLOSE stays beyond the level.

CRITERION 3: VOLUME
  vol_ratio on the breaking candle:
    > 1.5: strong volume confirmation → valid BOS
    1.0-1.5: adequate volume → valid BOS (less conviction)
    < 1.0: below-average volume → suspect BOS
      (low volume breakout may be a false break / inducement)

  When volume is low on a BOS candle, the system should:
    - Still acknowledge the BOS (close DID break)
    - But reduce confidence in the continuation
    - Look for volume confirmation on the NEXT candle
    - If next candle also has low volume → likely false break

CRITERION 4: FOLLOW-THROUGH
  After the BOS candle, the next 2-3 candles should confirm:
    - They remain beyond the broken level (no immediate rejection)
    - At least one shows continuation in the BOS direction

  If the candle after the BOS immediately reverses back below/above
  the broken level → the BOS may have been a fakeout.
  This does not invalidate it technically (the close DID break),
  but it dramatically reduces its reliability.

  The system checks follow-through by examining candles[break+1]
  through candles[break+3]:
    All close beyond the level → strong follow-through
    Some close beyond, some don't → moderate (consolidation)
    Immediate reversal back → weak (possible false break)

CRITERION 5: WHICH SWING WAS BROKEN
  The significance of a BOS depends on which swing was broken:

  Breaking a STRONG swing (quality 21+):
  → Major BOS. Very significant structural event.
  → High confidence that the trend continues.

  Breaking a MODERATE swing (quality 15-20):
  → Standard BOS. Normal trend continuation.

  Breaking a WEAK swing (quality 10-14):
  → Minor BOS. Less significant.
  → Could be noise, especially on LTF.

  Breaking a NOISE swing (quality <10):
  → Not meaningful BOS. Ignore for structural purposes.
  → This is inducement territory.

────────────────────────────────────────
BOS QUALITY SCORE
────────────────────────────────────────
The system rates each BOS event using 5 criteria:

  Factor 1: Broken swing quality (1-5)
    Noise swing = 1, Weak = 2, Moderate = 3, Strong = 4-5

  Factor 2: Breaking candle body_pct (1-5)
    <40% = 1, 40-50% = 2, 50-60% = 3, 60-75% = 4, >75% = 5

  Factor 3: Volume on break (1-5)
    <0.7× = 1, 0.7-1.0× = 2, 1.0-1.5× = 3, 1.5-2.0× = 4, >2.0× = 5

  Factor 4: Follow-through (1-5)
    Immediate reversal = 1, Mixed = 2-3, Partial = 3-4, Clean = 5

  Factor 5: Displacement present? (1-5)
    No displacement = 1-2
    Displacement Grade C = 3
    Displacement Grade B = 4
    Displacement Grade A = 5

  Total: 5-25
    20-25: Strong BOS → high confidence in continuation
    14-19: Standard BOS → normal confidence
    8-13: Weak BOS → low confidence, needs additional confirmation
    5-7: Questionable → likely false break or inducement

────────────────────────────────────────
WHAT HAPPENS AFTER A VALID BOS
────────────────────────────────────────
After a bullish BOS (new HH confirmed):
  1. Expect a pullback (price rarely goes straight up)
  2. The pullback should form a Higher Low (HL)
  3. The HL is expected to form near:
     - The FVG created by the BOS impulse
     - The OB that preceded the BOS impulse
     - The OTE zone (62-79% retracement of the BOS leg)
  4. This pullback IS the entry opportunity
     → Look for LTF confirmation at the pullback zone
     → This is Entry Model C (BOS Pullback)

After a bearish BOS (new LL confirmed):
  Mirror of above. Expect a rally (LH), then continuation down.

────────────────────────────────────────
BOS vs LIQUIDITY GRAB — THE CRITICAL DISTINCTION
────────────────────────────────────────
This is one of the most important distinctions in all of ICT.
Getting it wrong is costly.

BOS (real breakout — trend continues):
  ✓ Close breaks the level
  ✓ Body > 50% of range on the breaking candle
  ✓ Volume ≥ 1.0× average (ideally > 1.5×)
  ✓ Follow-through: next 2-3 candles stay beyond the level
  ✓ Usually accompanied by displacement
  ✓ Creates new OBs and FVGs in the breakout direction

LIQUIDITY GRAB (fake breakout — reversal coming):
  ✗ Wick breaks the level but close returns below/above
  ✗ OR close barely breaks, then immediate reversal next candle
  ✗ Volume may spike (stop triggers) but no follow-through
  ✗ Often followed by displacement in the OPPOSITE direction
  ✗ The "break" was the sweep — the real move is the reversal

When the system sees a level broken:
  Step 1: Did CLOSE break or just wick? → Close = potential BOS / Wick = likely sweep
  Step 2: What does the NEXT candle do?
    → Continues beyond = supports BOS interpretation
    → Reverses back = supports sweep interpretation
  Step 3: Is there displacement?
    → Displacement in breakout direction = BOS confirmed
    → Displacement in reversal direction = sweep confirmed
  Step 4: Volume pattern?
    → High volume on break + continued volume = BOS
    → Volume spike on break + volume drops = sweep (climactic)

The system should NOT make a final determination on the breaking
candle alone. Wait 1-3 candles for evidence. In live analysis,
this means: do not immediately call BOS. Say "level broken,
monitoring for confirmation" and update after follow-through data.

================================================================================
4.3  CHANGE OF CHARACTER (CHoCH)
================================================================================

CHoCH is the event that WARNS of a potential trend reversal.
It is not confirmation — it is the first signal that the current
trend may be ending.

────────────────────────────────────────
DEFINITION
────────────────────────────────────────
BULLISH CHoCH (potential reversal from bearish to bullish):
  In an existing downtrend (LH/LL structure):
  Price's CLOSE breaks ABOVE the most recent Lower High (LH).

  What this means: in a downtrend, price was making lower highs.
  Now it has broken above the last LH, meaning it just made a
  higher high relative to the last swing high. The LH/LL pattern
  may be breaking.

BEARISH CHoCH (potential reversal from bullish to bearish):
  In an existing uptrend (HH/HL structure):
  Price's CLOSE breaks BELOW the most recent Higher Low (HL).

  What this means: in an uptrend, price was making higher lows.
  Now it has broken below the last HL, meaning it just made a
  lower low relative to the last swing low. The HH/HL pattern
  may be breaking.

KEY DIFFERENCE FROM BOS:
  BOS = breaking a level WITH the trend → continuation
  CHoCH = breaking a level AGAINST the trend → potential reversal

  In uptrend: breaking above swing high = BOS (with trend)
              breaking below swing low = CHoCH (against trend)

  In downtrend: breaking below swing low = BOS (with trend)
                breaking above swing high = CHoCH (against trend)

────────────────────────────────────────
CHoCH IS A WARNING, NOT CONFIRMATION
────────────────────────────────────────
This is critical. Many traders (and many trading systems) treat
CHoCH as a confirmed reversal and immediately enter. This leads
to significant losses because:

  1. CHoCH can be a DEEP PULLBACK, not a reversal.
     In a strong uptrend, price may break below the last HL but
     then immediately resume the uptrend with a new HH. The HL
     break was a sweep of sell-side liquidity, not a trend change.

  2. CHoCH without displacement is especially suspect.
     If the HL was broken by a small candle with no volume and no
     displacement, the "break" may not represent institutional
     activity. It could be stop-hunting before continuation.

  3. The trend in force is statistically more likely to continue
     than to reverse. A CHoCH is fighting the prevailing trend,
     which means the burden of proof is on the reversal.

THE CONFIRMATION SEQUENCE:
  CHoCH alone is the first signal (warning level).
  For a full reversal confirmation, the sequence must be:

  For Bearish Reversal (uptrend → downtrend):
    Step 1: CHoCH → price breaks below the last HL ✓
    Step 2: Price rallies but forms a LOWER HIGH (LH)
            The rally cannot exceed the high that preceded the CHoCH
    Step 3: Price drops and breaks BELOW the low of the CHoCH move
            This is the first BOS in the new (bearish) direction
    Step 4: Now bearish structure is confirmed: you have LH + LL

    → Only at Step 3/4 is the reversal truly confirmed
    → At Step 1 alone, confidence in reversal is 40-50%
    → At Step 2 (LH formed), confidence rises to 55-65%
    → At Step 3 (BOS down), confidence rises to 70-80%

  For Bullish Reversal (downtrend → uptrend):
    Step 1: CHoCH → price breaks above the last LH ✓
    Step 2: Price pulls back but forms a HIGHER LOW (HL)
    Step 3: Price breaks ABOVE the high of the CHoCH move (BOS up)
    Step 4: Bullish structure confirmed: HH + HL

────────────────────────────────────────
CHoCH WITH vs WITHOUT DISPLACEMENT
────────────────────────────────────────
CHoCH with Displacement:
  The candle that breaks the structural level meets displacement
  criteria (range > 2× ATR, body > 70%, volume > 1.5×).

  This is a STRONG CHoCH. It means institutions actively drove
  price through the level with force. The probability of genuine
  reversal is significantly higher.

  Confidence adjustment: +15% over base CHoCH assessment

CHoCH without Displacement:
  The level was broken by a normal candle, or gradually across
  several small candles.

  This is a WEAK CHoCH. It could be:
    - A deep pullback (price may resume the original trend)
    - Stop-hunting / inducement (sweep the HL/LH stops, then reverse)
    - A genuine but slow reversal (needs more evidence)

  Confidence adjustment: +0% (base CHoCH assessment only)

  RULE: never enter a reversal trade on a weak CHoCH alone.
  Wait for the full confirmation sequence (LH→BOS or HL→BOS).

────────────────────────────────────────
CHoCH QUALITY SCORE
────────────────────────────────────────
  Factor 1: Which swing was broken? (1-5)
    Minor swing (N=2) = 1-2 (this might not be a real CHoCH at all)
    Moderate swing (N=3) = 3
    Strong swing (N=5) = 4-5 (genuine structural level broken)

  Factor 2: Displacement on the break? (1-5)
    No displacement = 1-2
    Grade C displacement = 3
    Grade B = 4, Grade A = 5

  Factor 3: Volume (1-5)
    Same scale as BOS volume scoring

  Factor 4: Location (1-5)
    CHoCH at a HTF POI (Daily/Weekly OB, FVG) = 5
    CHoCH at a HTF liquidity level = 4
    CHoCH at a moderate level (4H OB/FVG) = 3
    CHoCH in open space = 1-2

  Factor 5: Preceded by a sweep? (1-5)
    CHoCH immediately after SSL/BSL sweep = 5
    (sweep → displacement → CHoCH = classic ICT reversal pattern)
    CHoCH after gradual approach = 2-3
    CHoCH with no sweep = 1-2

  Total: 5-25
    20-25: Strong CHoCH → high probability reversal developing
    14-19: Standard CHoCH → moderate probability, wait for LH/HL
    8-13: Weak CHoCH → low probability, likely deep pullback
    5-7: Noise → not a real CHoCH, probably minor swing break

────────────────────────────────────────
CHoCH ON DIFFERENT TIMEFRAMES
────────────────────────────────────────
The TIMEFRAME of the CHoCH dramatically affects its significance:

  Weekly CHoCH: Extremely rare. When it happens, the entire market
    character changes for months. Highest significance possible.

  Daily CHoCH: Rare. Signals a multi-day or multi-week potential
    reversal. Changes the Daily Bias. This is what triggers the
    HTF Reversal Trade exception (see SYSTEM_CORE Rule 4).

  4H CHoCH: Moderately common. Signals an intraday or multi-day
    structural shift. Changes the 4H context. If aligned with
    Daily bias → strong signal. If against Daily bias → caution
    (may be a deep pullback).

  1H CHoCH: Common. Most relevant for intraday trading. This is
    the "get ready" signal at a HTF zone:
    - Price reaches a 4H OB or FVG
    - 1H prints a CHoCH in the trade direction
    - → Now look at 15m for entry confirmation

  15m/5m CHoCH: Very common. Used for entry timing.
    - 15m CHoCH at a 1H/4H zone = the entry trigger
    - This is the MSS (Market Structure Shift) on the entry TF
    - 5m CHoCH is used for sniper entry refinement

  RULE: A LTF CHoCH never overrides a HTF trend.
    1H CHoCH bearish while Daily is bullish → this is a pullback
    on the 1H within the Daily uptrend. It is NOT a sell signal.
    It IS a warning to wait before entering new longs.
    Once 1H structure flips back bullish → resume buying.

================================================================================
4.3B  CHANGE IN STATE OF DELIVERY (CISD) — THE CANDLE-BODY EARLY SIGNAL
================================================================================

⚠️ ROOT-CAUSE ADDITION (July 2026, after deep external research into
Michael Huddleston's actual methodology): this concept was completely
missing from the constitution before this update, despite being one
of the three sequential reversal confirmations ICT teaches (IFVG/BPR
weakness → CISD → MSS strength, each one a progressively stronger and
later-arriving confirmation of the same directional shift). Without
CISD, the system could only see the LATEST and STRONGEST signal (MSS)
and had no vocabulary for the EARLIER, WEAKER hint that price delivery
is changing — meaning entries were systematically late, missing the
setups where CISD fired cleanly days or hours before the MSS.

────────────────────────────────────────
WHAT CISD ACTUALLY MEASURES (AND WHY IT DIFFERS FROM MSS)
────────────────────────────────────────
Both CISD and MSS answer the same underlying question — "has the
direction of price delivery just changed?" — but they look at
completely different parts of the candle to answer it:

  MSS (Market Structure Shift): looks at the HIGH and LOW of candles.
    It asks: "has price closed beyond a swing high/low that itself
    represents a genuine liquidity sweep of an even older swing?"
    This requires an established swing point (which itself requires
    several candles of confirmation) — so MSS is, by construction,
    a LATE signal. It is also the STRONGEST signal, because breaking
    an actual structural swing point requires real conviction.

  CISD (Change in State of Delivery): looks at the OPEN and CLOSE of
    candles only — ignores wicks entirely. It asks: "has a run of
    same-direction candles (a 'delivery') just been invalidated by a
    close beyond the OPENING PRICE of that entire run?" This does not
    require any swing point at all — just a sequence of same-colored
    candles followed by one that closes past where that sequence
    began. Because it needs no swing confirmation, CISD can fire
    several candles BEFORE the corresponding MSS — but it is also
    weaker and more prone to false signals (a single counter-close
    that goes nowhere) than MSS.

This is exactly analogous to the OHLC_PROCESSING distinction between
"body" (open/close, what buyers/sellers actually committed to) and
"wick" (high/low, a level tested but rejected/not held) — CISD is a
pure "body-logic" concept; MSS is a "wick+body" (structural) concept.

────────────────────────────────────────
HOW TO IDENTIFY A "PRICE DELIVERY RUN" FROM RAW CANDLES
────────────────────────────────────────
A "bullish delivery run" is simply one or more CONSECUTIVE bullish
candles (close > open for each). A "bearish delivery run" is one or
more consecutive bearish candles (close < open for each). A run can
be a single candle — it does not need multiple candles to qualify,
though longer runs (3+ candles of the same color) represent a more
committed delivery and produce a more meaningful CISD when broken.

The KEY REFERENCE LEVEL of any delivery run is the OPEN of the FIRST
candle in that run — not the open of the most recent candle, and not
any high/low. This is the single most common calculation mistake:
grabbing the open of the last candle in the run instead of the first.

  Example — identifying a bullish delivery run's reference level:
    candle[i-4]: O:100.0 C:99.5   (bearish — this ends the PRIOR run)
    candle[i-3]: O:99.5  C:100.2  (bullish — run starts HERE)
    candle[i-2]: O:100.2 C:101.0  (bullish — run continues)
    candle[i-1]: O:101.0 C:101.8  (bullish — run continues)
    candle[i]:   O:101.8 C:102.3  (bullish — run continues, 4 candles so far)

  The bullish delivery run's reference level is 99.5 — the OPEN of
  candle[i-3], the FIRST candle in this run of 4 consecutive bullish
  candles. This is the level a bearish CISD must close below to
  invalidate this entire delivery run.

────────────────────────────────────────
BEARISH CISD — EXACT DETECTION RULE
────────────────────────────────────────
A Bearish CISD fires when:
  1. There exists a run of one or more consecutive BULLISH candles
     (the "bullish delivery"), with reference level = OPEN of the
     first candle in that run.
  2. A LATER candle's CLOSE (not wick — the close specifically) falls
     BELOW that reference level.

  Numeric example:
    Bullish delivery run: candle[i-3] through candle[i] as above
    (reference level = 99.5, the open of candle[i-3]).

    candle[i+1]: O:102.3 H:102.5 L:98.9 C:99.0

    Check: is candle[i+1].close (99.0) < reference level (99.5)?
    99.0 < 99.5 → YES → Bearish CISD confirmed at candle[i+1].

    Note that candle[i+1]'s wick (low 98.9) went even lower than its
    close (99.0) — but CISD only cares about the CLOSE. The wick
    reaching 98.9 is irrelevant to the CISD test itself (it may be
    relevant to other concepts like a liquidity sweep, evaluated
    separately).

  What this means in plain language: buyers had been in full control
  for 4 straight candles (bullish delivery). Now, in a single candle,
  sellers pushed price all the way back through where that entire
  4-candle rally began. This is a strong hint (not proof) that
  selling pressure has taken over — the "state of delivery" changed
  from bullish to bearish.

────────────────────────────────────────
BULLISH CISD — EXACT DETECTION RULE (mirror image)
────────────────────────────────────────
A Bullish CISD fires when:
  1. There exists a run of one or more consecutive BEARISH candles
     (the "bearish delivery"), with reference level = OPEN of the
     first candle in that run.
  2. A LATER candle's CLOSE rises ABOVE that reference level.

  Numeric example:
    candle[i-2]: O:105.0 C:104.2  (bearish — run starts here, ref=105.0)
    candle[i-1]: O:104.2 C:103.5  (bearish — run continues)
    candle[i]:   O:103.5 C:102.8  (bearish — run continues, 3 candles)
    candle[i+1]: O:102.8 H:105.4 L:102.6 C:105.2

    Check: is candle[i+1].close (105.2) > reference level (105.0)?
    105.2 > 105.0 → YES → Bullish CISD confirmed at candle[i+1].

────────────────────────────────────────
WHY CISD CAN "FAIL" MORE OFTEN THAN MSS (A REAL LIMITATION, NOT A FLAW)
────────────────────────────────────────
Because CISD requires only ONE candle's close to cross ONE open price
— with no requirement that this level represents any structurally
significant swing point, no requirement for displacement, and no
requirement for a prior liquidity sweep — it is mathematically much
easier to trigger than MSS. This means:

  - A single strong counter-trend candle can trigger CISD and then
    price simply resumes the original direction on the very next
    candle. This is a FAILED CISD — common, and not a system error;
    it is an inherent property of a fast, body-only signal.
  - CISD fires MORE OFTEN than genuine reversals actually occur. Do
    NOT treat every CISD as an entry trigger by itself.

WHEN CISD IS MOST RELIABLE (raise confidence, do not treat as gate):
  - CISD occurs immediately AFTER a genuine liquidity sweep (see
    LIQUIDITY_MAPPING) of a real external level — this is the
    classic "sweep then CISD" reversal sequence taught across ICT
    material, and it converts far more often than an isolated CISD
    in the middle of a range with no sweep behind it.
  - The candle causing the CISD itself meets displacement criteria
    (see MARKET_STRUCTURE 4.2/ORDER_BLOCKS 5.1 displacement rules) —
    a CISD candle that is ALSO a genuine displacement candle carries
    real institutional weight; a CISD candle that is small and
    unremarkable is a much weaker signal.
  - The CISD direction agrees with the higher-timeframe bias already
    established (Daily/4H) — a CISD against the HTF bias is far more
    likely to be a failed counter-trend blip than a genuine reversal.

────────────────────────────────────────
CISD vs MSS vs BOS vs CHoCH — WHEN TO USE WHICH (SUMMARY TABLE)
────────────────────────────────────────
  BOS: continuation signal — price breaks a swing point WITH the
    existing trend. Confirms the trend is intact, not reversing.

  CHoCH: the SOFTER reversal warning — price closes beyond the most
    recent COUNTER-trend swing point (a wick/high-low concept, no
    sweep or displacement strictly required by the base definition —
    see MARKET_STRUCTURE 4.3 above for the full confirmation sequence
    ICT requires before trusting it).

  CISD: the EARLIEST reversal hint — price closes beyond the OPEN of
    the most recent same-direction candle run (a body-only concept,
    ignores wicks entirely, does not need any swing point to exist).
    Fires potentially before CHoCH or MSS, but least reliable alone.

  MSS: the STRONGEST reversal confirmation — price closes beyond a
    swing point that itself resulted from a genuine liquidity sweep
    of an OLDER swing (see MARKET_STRUCTURE + LIQUIDITY_MAPPING for
    the full mechanical sweep-then-break sequence this requires).
    Fires latest, but is the most trustworthy on its own.

  A complete, textbook ICT reversal sequence often shows all three in
  order: CISD fires first (early warning) → CHoCH fires next (the
  counter-trend swing breaks) → MSS fires last (that same break is
  confirmed to have been preceded by a genuine sweep). The system
  should read ALL THREE where available and build one coherent
  narrative from them — not treat them as competing or contradictory
  signals. If they disagree (e.g. CISD says bullish but the broader
  structure still shows no CHoCH/MSS at all), that disagreement itself
  is meaningful: it means the reversal is only in its earliest,
  least-confirmed stage — narrate this honestly rather than picking
  one signal arbitrarily.

────────────────────────────────────────
WHERE CISD IS USED FOR ENTRY (NOT JUST BIAS)
────────────────────────────────────────
After a CISD confirms, the candle(s) that formed the counter-trend
delivery run typically leave behind their own Order Block and/or Fair
Value Gap (see ORDER_BLOCKS, FAIR_VALUE_GAPS) — because a CISD close
is, by definition, often a displacement-like move. The highest
probability entry is not AT the CISD candle itself (that is chasing
an already-extended move) but on the RETRACEMENT back into the OB/FVG
that the CISD-triggering candle(s) created — this mirrors exactly how
OB/FVG retracement entries work elsewhere in this constitution, CISD
simply supplies an earlier-arriving directional bias to trade that
retracement in.


================================================================================
4.4  INTERNAL vs EXTERNAL STRUCTURE
================================================================================

This concept is essential for understanding how multiple timeframes
interact and for avoiding the trap of trading a pullback as if it
were a reversal.

────────────────────────────────────────
DEFINITIONS
────────────────────────────────────────
EXTERNAL STRUCTURE:
  The structure visible on the HIGHER timeframes (Weekly, Daily, 4H).
  This is the dominant trend — the "parent" structure.
  External structure determines your BIAS (which direction to trade).
  External structure changes slowly and infrequently.

INTERNAL STRUCTURE:
  The structure visible on LOWER timeframes (1H, 15m, 5m) WITHIN
  the context of the external structure.
  Internal structure shows the detailed movement within each leg
  of the external structure.
  Internal structure changes frequently — and this is NORMAL.

────────────────────────────────────────
HOW THEY INTERACT
────────────────────────────────────────
Scenario 1: Internal AGREES with External (trend leg)
  External: Bullish (Daily HH/HL)
  Internal: Bullish (1H HH/HL)

  Meaning: price is in an impulse leg moving with the larger trend.
  Action: look for pullback entries on the internal structure.
  This is the highest-confidence environment for entries.

Scenario 2: Internal DISAGREES with External (pullback)
  External: Bullish (Daily HH/HL)
  Internal: Bearish (1H LH/LL)

  Meaning: price is pulling back within the larger uptrend.
  The 1H is making lower highs and lower lows, but this movement
  is WITHIN the Daily uptrend. It is a corrective move, not a
  reversal (until proven otherwise).

  Action:
    - Do NOT sell just because 1H is bearish
    - Wait for internal structure to realign with external
    - When 1H prints CHoCH bullish (breaking above the last LH)
      followed by BOS bullish → internal has realigned
    - NOW look for buy entries on 15m/5m
    - This is exactly the ICT entry sequence:
      HTF bullish → price pulls back → 1H flips back bullish at
      a HTF demand zone → enter on LTF confirmation

  This scenario IS your primary trading opportunity.

Scenario 3: Internal breaks External (potential reversal)
  External: Bullish (Daily HH/HL)
  Internal: Bearish (1H LH/LL) AND 1H breaks below the Daily HL

  Meaning: the internal bearish structure has now broken the
  external structure's key level (the Daily HL). This is a
  CHoCH on the Daily timeframe.

  This is significant. The internal structure is no longer just
  a pullback — it has violated the parent structure.

  Action:
    - Daily bias becomes uncertain
    - Do NOT enter new longs
    - Watch for Daily to form a LH (confirming the CHoCH)
    - If Daily forms LH + BOS down → new bearish external structure
    - If Daily recovers above the HL → the break was a sweep (false)

────────────────────────────────────────
PRACTICAL APPLICATION RULES
────────────────────────────────────────
RULE 1: Never trade internal structure against external structure.
  If external is bullish, only take bullish internal setups.
  Bearish internal moves are pullbacks — wait them out.

RULE 2: Internal CHoCH against external is a WAIT signal, not SELL.
  1H turns bearish while Daily is bullish → do not sell.
  Wait for 1H to turn bullish again, then look for entries.

RULE 3: Internal BOS against external is a WARNING signal.
  1H BOS bearish while Daily is bullish → something may be changing.
  Check: is price near a major external level (Daily/Weekly OB)?
  If yes → potential external reversal forming (HTF Reversal Trade?)
  If no → likely a deep pullback, still wait for internal realignment.

RULE 4: When internal realigns with external → highest probability.
  External bullish + Internal just flipped back to bullish
  (after being bearish during pullback) = the sweet spot.
  This is where Entry Models A, B, C are most effective.

────────────────────────────────────────
IDENTIFYING INTERNAL vs EXTERNAL IN THE DATA
────────────────────────────────────────
The system determines which swings are external and which are
internal based on timeframe:

  If analyzing on 1H:
    External swings = 4H and Daily swings (imported or constructed)
    Internal swings = 1H swings between the external swings

  If analyzing on 15m:
    External swings = 1H and 4H swings
    Internal swings = 15m swings

  The system must track which external swings define the current
  external structure and ensure that internal swing breaks are
  not confused with external structure breaks.

  Practical check:
    When a 1H swing low breaks on a bearish candle:
      - Is this swing low also a 4H swing low? → External break (significant)
      - Is it only a 1H swing low between two 4H swings? → Internal break (pullback)

================================================================================
4.5  STRUCTURE ON EACH TIMEFRAME — WHAT TO EXTRACT
================================================================================

Each timeframe provides specific structural information. The system
processes them in order (top-down) and builds a composite picture.

────────────────────────────────────────
WEEKLY STRUCTURE EXTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
From the Weekly swing sequence, determine:
  □ Overall trend: bullish (HH/HL), bearish (LH/LL), or ranging
  □ Current position within the trend:
    - After a swing high (potentially pulling back)
    - After a swing low (potentially starting new impulse)
    - Near the middle (between key swings)
  □ Distance to last major swing high and low
  □ Last structural event (BOS or CHoCH) and when it occurred
  □ Strength of the current structure (strong or weakening?)

Output: WEEKLY_TREND = {direction, strength, last_event, position}

────────────────────────────────────────
DAILY STRUCTURE EXTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
From the Daily swing sequence:
  □ Daily trend direction and strength
  □ Last Daily BOS or CHoCH (this is the DAILY BIAS)
  □ Current internal position:
    - Is price in an impulse leg or pullback?
    - How deep is the current pullback?
    - Has the current impulse shown signs of exhaustion?
  □ Key Daily swing levels (last SH and SL prices)
  □ Is Daily structure aligned with Weekly?
    - Yes → strong bias, high confidence
    - No → caution, possible transition

Output: DAILY_BIAS = {direction, confidence, last_event,
                      key_levels, weekly_alignment}

────────────────────────────────────────
4H STRUCTURE EXTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
From the 4H swing sequence:
  □ 4H trend direction and strength
  □ Last 4H BOS/CHoCH
  □ Is 4H aligned with Daily?
    - Yes → look for entries (context supports)
    - No → is it a pullback within Daily trend?
      - Yes → wait for 4H to realign (entry opportunity forming)
      - No (breaking Daily structure) → caution, bias may change
  □ Key 4H swing levels
  □ 4H OBs and FVGs within the current structure

Output: FOUR_H_CONTEXT = {direction, daily_alignment,
                          pullback_status, key_zones}

────────────────────────────────────────
1H STRUCTURE EXTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
From the 1H swing sequence:
  □ 1H trend direction
  □ Last 1H BOS/CHoCH
  □ Is 1H aligned with 4H?
  □ Is price currently at or approaching a HTF zone?
  □ Has a CHoCH/BOS just occurred at a HTF zone?
    → If yes → ENTRY_READY
    → If no → WAIT

Output: ONE_H_STATUS = {direction, entry_ready, at_htf_zone,
                        last_structural_event}

────────────────────────────────────────
15m / 5m STRUCTURE EXTRACTION
────────────────────────────────────────
Only processed when 1H says ENTRY_READY.

  □ Has MSS (CHoCH) occurred on 15m/5m in the trade direction?
  □ Has BOS confirmed the MSS?
  □ Where is the LTF OB/FVG formed from the MSS/BOS?
  □ SL level: nearest structural swing on the entry TF

Output: ENTRY_SIGNAL = {confirmed, entry_level, sl_level,
                        confirmation_type}

================================================================================
4.6  INDUCEMENT — THE TRAP OF FALSE BREAKS
================================================================================

Inducement is the intentional or incidental break of a MINOR
structural level to lure traders into the wrong direction before
the real move occurs.

────────────────────────────────────────
HOW INDUCEMENT WORKS
────────────────────────────────────────
In an uptrend (HH/HL):
  Between two major HLs, there are minor swing lows.
  When price breaks below a minor swing low:
    - Retail traders see "trend break" and go short
    - Stop losses of existing longs (placed below minor swings) trigger
    - Smart Money collects this liquidity (the short entries and the
      triggered long stops are sell orders = liquidity for SM to buy)
    - Price then reverses and continues the uptrend
    - Retail shorts get stopped out, retail longs were already stopped

  The minor swing break was INDUCEMENT:
    - It induced retail to take the wrong side
    - It provided liquidity for institutional buying
    - The major HL was never threatened

────────────────────────────────────────
DETECTING INDUCEMENT FROM OHLC DATA
────────────────────────────────────────
Step 1: Identify the major structural swings (N=3+ on the analysis TF,
        or MODERATE+ quality)

Step 2: Identify minor swings between them (N=2, or WEAK quality)

Step 3: When a minor swing breaks:
  Check: is the major swing (below for uptrend, above for downtrend)
  still intact?
    - YES → this is likely inducement, not real structural break
    - NO → the major swing also broke → this IS a real break (CHoCH/BOS)

Step 4: If likely inducement:
  - Do NOT change structural assessment
  - Watch for reversal back in the trend direction
  - If reversal occurs with displacement → inducement confirmed
  - The zone around the minor swing break is now an entry opportunity:
    price swept the minor liquidity, institutional orders filled,
    ready to continue the trend

────────────────────────────────────────
INDUCEMENT vs CHoCH — THE DECISIVE QUESTION
────────────────────────────────────────
When a swing low breaks in an uptrend, the system must determine:
Is this inducement (minor break, trend continues) or CHoCH (major
break, trend may be reversing)?

Decision criteria:
  1. WHICH swing broke?
     Minor (N=2, weak quality) → likely inducement
     Major (N=3+, moderate+ quality) → likely CHoCH

  2. Displacement on the break?
